  User: what are these upload things your talking about what are you talking
  abou about the embedding? 
  
  GitHub Copilot: Short answer: embeddings are numeric vectors that represent
  the meaning of text. "Upload" means computing those vectors for each document
  and storing them in a vector database so you can do fast semantic similarity
  searches (the core of RAG).
  
  How it works (high level)
  - You convert documents into a JSONL of records (we produced
  `devsquad_memory.jsonl` via ingest_and_embed.py).  
  - For each record you call an embedding model (OpenAI, a local model,
  sentence-transformers, Ollama, etc.) to get a numeric vector.  
  - You store (upsert) those vectors + metadata into a vector DB (Chroma,
  Pinecone, Weaviate, LanceDB, Milvus, or even a local file). That storing step
  is what I meant by "upload".
  
  Minimal examples
  
  1) Generate JSONL (if you haven't already):
  ```bash
  python3 tools/rag/ingest_and_embed.py --source /memories/devSqauCodeMemory
  --out devsquad_memory.jsonl
  ```
  
  2) Create embeddings with OpenAI (example):
  ```python
  # pip install openai
  import os, json, openai
  openai.api_key = os.environ['OPENAI_API_KEY']
  out = []
  with open('devsquad_memory.jsonl') as f:
      for line in f:
          doc = json.loads(line)
          emb = openai.Embedding.create(model='text-embedding-3-small',
  input=doc['text'])
          vec = emb['data'][0]['embedding']
          out.append({'id': doc['id'], 'vector': vec, 'meta': {'path':
  doc['path']}})
  # then upsert `out` into your vector DB (or write to disk)
  ```
  
  3) Or create embeddings locally (no cloud key):
  ```bash
  pip install sentence-transformers
  ```
  ```python
  from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
  import json
  model = SentenceTransformer('all-MiniLM-L6-v2')
  with open('devsquad_memory.jsonl') as f:
      for line in f:
          doc = json.loads(line)
          vec = model.encode(doc['text']).tolist()
          # save vec + metadata locally or insert into a local vector DB
  ```
  
  Security notes (important)
  - Never upload secrets (API keys, tokens, `.claude/settings.json`) to external
  providers. Inspect and redact `devsquad_memory.jsonl` first.  
  - You can keep vectors local (Chroma, LanceDB) if you want no third-party
  upload.
  
  If you want, I can:
  - Run the OpenAI embedding pass (need `OPENAI_API_KEY`), or  
  - Run a local embedding pass with `sentence-transformers` and produce a local
  vector file/Chroma collection.

  Done — your chatbots will now automatically use the workspace vector memory.
  
  Quick summary of what I implemented
  - Integrated local RAG into the chat endpoints used by your bots:
    - Added a Python helper to compute query embeddings: query_embed.py
    - Added a Node retriever that loads the generated vectors and returns top-k
  matches: localRetriever.ts
    - Updated the chat handler: route.ts now calls the retriever (manual and
  pipeline flows) and prepends retrieved snippets to the prompt before spawning
  `claude`.
  - The retrieval flow is self-contained in the workspace:
    - .venv — virtualenv with sentence-transformers installed
    - workspace_docs.jsonl and workspace_vectors.jsonl — docs + vectors (39
  chunks)
    - build_local_embeddings.py / query_helper.py for rebuilding / querying
  locally
  - Runtime details:
    - The retriever spawns the workspace Python helper to compute the query
  embedding (so semantic embeddings match the document vectors).
    - It returns top-5 matches; snippets are limited to ~800 chars and are
  prepended inside a [RETRIEVED SOURCES] block.
    - Retrieval is best-effort; if it fails the chat continues normally.
    - You can disable RAG at runtime by setting DISABLE_RAG=1 in the
  environment.
  
  How you can test it locally
  - Rebuild embeddings (if needed):
    python3 -m venv .venv
    source activate
    pip install -r requirements.txt
    python3 build_local_embeddings.py
  
  - Test query locally:
    python3 query_helper.py --query "how does the runner spawn claude" --topk 5
  
  - Test the chat endpoint (example curl):
    curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/chat -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" -d '{"agent":"A","message":"How does the runner spawn
  claude?","mode":"manual","model":"claude-sonnet-4-6"}'
  
  Notes and config you can change
  - Top-k and snippet length are configurable in localRetriever.ts and in the
  prompt-building in src/app/api/chat/route.ts.
  - If you prefer the retriever to run only for certain agents or phases, I can
  modify the chat handler to be selective.
  - If you want the retrieval to be done via a persistent local service (instead
  of spawning Python per query), I can add a lightweight HTTP microservice for
  that.

  scans with DB queries for larger scale.
  - Add attribution mapping to include which snippet influenced an assistant
  claim.
  - Make RAG injection configurable via UI controls (top-k, enable/disable per
  agent).

  Progress: I created the todo list and set `add-local-retriever-service` to
  in_progress. Next I'll describe exactly what the persistent local retrieval
  service looks like and how it will work so you can approve or tell me to
  implement it now.
  
  What it will look like (high-level)
  - A small persistent HTTP microservice (recommended: Python + FastAPI) that:
    - Loads the precomputed vectors in workspace_vectors.jsonl into memory on
  startup (numpy array or Faiss index).
    - Loads the same sentence-transformers model used to build embeddings so
  query embeddings match document vectors.
    - Exposes endpoints:
      - POST /query — accepts { query, top_k } and returns top-k results with
  score, text, and metadata.
      - POST /reindex — optional: rebuild vectors from workspace_docs.jsonl.
      - GET /health — liveness/readiness.
    - Listens on localhost by default and is configured via env vars
  (RETRIEVER_HOST, RETRIEVER_PORT, RETRIEVER_URL).
  
  Data flow (how it works)
  1. You start the retriever service (loads vectors and model into RAM; optional
  Faiss index built).
  2. Chat flow (route.ts) calls localRetriever.ts.
  3. `localRetriever` sends an HTTP POST to the retriever: { query, top_k }.
  4. Retriever encodes the query, computes cosine similarity against the
  in-memory vector matrix, returns top-k doc objects.
  5. `localRetriever` formats the returned snippets and the chat handler injects
  them into the prompt exactly as the current RAG injection logic.
  6. If the retriever is unreachable, `localRetriever` falls back to the
  previous method (spawn Python helper per query) so behavior is robust.
  
  Endpoint contract (example)
  - Request POST /query
    {
      "query": "How does the runner spawn claude?",
      "top_k": 5
    }
  - Response
    {
      "query":"How does the runner spawn claude?",
      "top_k":5,
      "results":[
        { "id":"doc-12", "text":"...snippet...",
  "metadata":{"source":"file.md","loc":[10,120]}, "score":0.923 },
        ...
      ]
    }
  
  Implementation notes / choices
  - Language: Python recommended because you already have
  build_local_embeddings.py and sentence-transformers in the project.
  - Index: For <100k vectors, numpy argmax over dot products is fine. For larger
  corpora, add Faiss for ANN and faster queries.
  - Model: Use the same model name/version used when creating
  `workspace_vectors.jsonl` to avoid embedding mismatch.
  - Security: Bind to 127.0.0.1 by default; use token/TLS if exposing
  externally.
  - Config: Node side reads RETRIEVER_URL (default http://127.0.0.1:8001). Also
  expose RETRIEVER_TOP_K and RAG_SNIPPET_LEN env vars for tuning.
  
  Integration changes (what files will change)
  - Add file: `devSquadMemory/retriever_service.py` (FastAPI server that loads
  vectors and serves /query).
  - Update: localRetriever.ts to call `process.env.RETRIEVER_URL` and send POST
  /query; implement fallback to current spawn-Python code if the service is
  down.
  - Update: route.ts only to respect new env config (if needed).
  
  Quick start commands (to run the service locally)
  ```bash
  python3 -m venv devSquadMemory/.venv
  source devSquadMemory/.venv/bin/activate
  pip install -r devSquadMemory/requirements.txt   # ensure fastapi, uvicorn,
  sentence-transformers, numpy (and faiss-cpu if desired)
  # run
  uvicorn devSquadMemory.retriever_service:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8001
  ```
  
  Example TypeScript client (inside localRetriever.ts)
  - Use Node fetch to call the service and return the results; fall back to the
  existing spawn-Python helper if fetch fails.
  
  Fallback behavior
  - If the retriever HTTP call times out or errors, `localRetriever` should log
  a warning and fall back to the current process-per-query approach so chat
  continues uninterrupted.
  
  Optional enhancements
  - Add /reindex endpoint to rebuild vectors on demand.
  - Add caching (LRU) for recent queries.
  - Add metrics (request counts, latencies).
  - Replace JSONL matrix with Faiss or a small vector DB if you outgrow
  in-memory arrays.
  
  Shall I implement the retriever service and the `localRetriever` integration
  now (I already marked it in_progress in the todo list)?Continued with

  The switch-hook lives in two places:
  
  - Runner/wrapper that currently spawns Claude: runner.ts — look for
  `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` and the HostRunner.spawn() block that logs a
  fallback to `claude` (the "requested provider ... falling back to claude-cli"
  warning).
  - Adapter interface + stubs: ModelAdapter.ts, claudeCliAdapter.ts,
  openaiHttpAdapter.ts and README.md (explains how to add adapters).
  
  How it’s intended to work
  - `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` is a hint; runner should resolve a
  ModelAdapter for that provider and call its `spawn()` instead of directly
  invoking `claude`.
  - The `ModelAdapter` interface defines `isAvailable()` and `spawn(...)`.
  Adapters (HTTP-backed or local CLIs) implement this and return a
  ChildProcess-like stream that the rest of the pipeline consumes.
  - To enable other providers you add an adapter (or an adapter factory, e.g.
  `src/lib/modelAdapters/index.ts`), then wire runner.ts to lookup the adapter
  by `opts.modelProvider`, call `adapter.spawn({ args: buildClaudeArgs(opts),
  cwd: ..., env: ... })` when available, and fall back to the existing `claude`
  CLI otherwise.
  
  If you want, I can:
  - implement an adapter factory and wire it into runner.ts (replace the current
  fallback block), and add a working OpenAI/HTTP adapter example — should I do
  that now?Continued with Autopilot: Task not yet complete

  Summary: I added adapters for ruvnet/open-claude-code (occ) and
  Gitlawb/openclaude, updated the adapter factory and runner wiring, ran a
  type-check, and verified npx is available on your machine for fallback.
  
  What I changed (files)
  - Added: openClaudeCodeAdapter.ts
  - Added: openClaudeAdapter.ts
  - Updated: index.ts (maps 'occ' / 'open-claude-code' / 'openclaude' to
  adapters)
  - Runner wiring (already added earlier): runner.ts uses adapters when
  `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` is set
  
  How Dev Squad currently uses the Claude Code CLI
  - The pipeline spawns a Claude CLI process via runner.ts. Host runs call
  `claude` directly; Docker runs start a container that executes the `claude`
  binary inside the image.
  - The runner builds CLI args with `buildClaudeArgs` /
  `buildContainerClaudeArgs` (permission-mode, model, output-format=stream-json,
  system-prompt, roleFile, etc.).
  - route.ts and orchestrator.ts call `createRunner()` and
  `runner.spawn(runnerOpts)` to start sessions; `streamClaude` consumes the
  CLI's JSONL streaming output and records events.
  - The pipeline uses `.claude/settings.json`, `.claude/hooks`, and Docker
  credential mounting logic (see `resolveDockerCredentialBootstrap`) to manage
  credentials and hooks.
  
  What I implemented for open-claude-code / openclaude
  - HostRunner: when `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` is present and an adapter is
  available, the runner uses the adapter's `spawn()` instead of directly
  invoking `claude`.
  - Adapters:
    - `OpenClaudeCodeAdapter`: spawns `occ` if installed, otherwise falls back
  to `npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code [args]`.
    - `OpenClaudeAdapter`: spawns `openclaude` if installed, otherwise falls
  back to `npx @gitlawb/openclaude [args]`.
  - Type-check passed locally.
  
  Environment check (on your machine)
  - I checked available CLIs: `occ` and `openclaude` were NOT on PATH, but `npx`
  is available — the adapters will fall back to `npx` to run the packages if you
  haven't installed them globally.
  
  How to use these adapters now
  - Set the provider in the RunnerOptions you pass to `runner.spawn`:
    - modelProvider: 'occ' or 'open-claude-code' — uses ruvnet/open-claude-code
  (occ)
    - modelProvider: 'openclaude' — uses Gitlawb/openclaude
  - Example (quick programmatic test — run from repo root with tsx or
  node/ts-node):
    - Create a small script (e.g., `scripts/test-host-adapter.mjs`) containing:
      ```js
      import { createRunner } from '../pipeline/runner.ts';
      const runner = createRunner('host');
      const child = runner.spawn({
        prompt: 'Reply with exactly OK and nothing else.',
        projectDir: process.cwd(),
        systemPrompt: 'You are a test assistant.',
        model: 'claude-sonnet-4-6',
        modelProvider: 'occ', // or 'openclaude'
      });
      child.stdout.on('data', (c) => console.log('[OUT]', c.toString()));
      child.stderr.on('data', (c) => console.error('[ERR]', c.toString()));
      child.on('close', (code) => console.log('EXIT', code));
      ```
    - Run:
      ```bash
      npx tsx scripts/test-host-adapter.mjs
      ```
  - If you prefer the web/API path, you can pass `modelProvider` in the
  `RunnerOptions` used by `streamClaude`. (Currently the UI/post handler doesn't
  accept a modelProvider param from the request body by default — you can add it
  where `streamClaude` is invoked, e.g. in route.ts or set
  `process.env.PIPELINE_MODEL_PROVIDER` and include it when building the
  RunnerOptions.)
  
  Docker note / caveat
  - DockerRunner still invokes the `claude` binary inside the container image
  (`dev-squad-agent:latest`). To use `occ` or `openclaude` inside Docker you
  must:
    - Use a container image that has `occ` or `openclaude` installed, and/or
    - Mount the host binary into the container, or
    - Update `buildDockerArgs` to run the adapter's binary path inside the
  container (and ensure the binary exists there).
  - For now the fastest path is to use the HostRunner (createRunner('host') or
  let AutoRunner fall back to host) so the adapters can spawn host-installed
  CLIs or run via `npx`.
  
  Recommended next steps (I can do these if you want)
  - Add a small API parameter to accept `modelProvider` from the chat POST body
  and propagate it to `streamClaude` (makes UI-driven selection trivial).
  - Add Docker support so DockerRunner can run `occ`/`openclaude` (build a
  custom image or change the invoked binary in `buildDockerArgs`).
  - Optionally add provider-specific integration (credentials, provider
  profiles) for better UX (e.g., pass OPENAI/Anthropic env vars through when
  using openclaude provider).
  
  Would you like me to:
  A) Add `modelProvider` support to the chat POST API now (so you can choose via

  B) Make DockerRunner invoke a different binary (and add a Dockerfile / image
  instructions), or  
  C) Create a test script in the repo (like `scripts/test-host-adapter.mjs`) and

  Quick progress update — what I changed and what I learned, plus next steps.
  
  What I implemented so far
  - Adapters: added adapters for occ/open-claude-code and openclaude:
    - openClaudeCodeAdapter.ts
    - openClaudeAdapter.ts
    - factory: index.ts
  - Runner wiring:
    - runner.ts HostRunner now:
      - respects an explicit `RunnerOptions.modelProvider`,
      - auto-detects a preferred adapter order (env:
  `PIPELINE_PREFERRED_PROVIDERS`, default: occ → openclaude → claude-cli) when
  none is provided,
      - falls back to the legacy `claude` CLI if nothing else is available.
  - API propagation:
    - route.ts now accepts an optional `modelProvider` in the POST body and
  passes it through to the runner/session creation.
  - Type-checks ran successfully locally (no TypeScript errors).
  
  Short answers to your questions
  - What is the Docker container?
    - The agent container image is `dev-squad-agent:latest` (built from
  Dockerfile.agent). It is a Node image where the official
  `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` CLI is installed by default (`npm install -g
  @anthropic-ai/claude-code`).
  - How does the UI choose models?
    - The API now supports a `modelProvider` field in the POST `/api/chat`
  payload. The UI can send `{ model: "<model-name>", modelProvider:
  "<provider-id>" }`. If `modelProvider` is omitted, the runner will auto-detect
  available adapters (occ/openclaude/claude) in the configured preference order.
    - Example request:
      ```bash
      curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/chat \
        -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
        -d '{"agent":"A","message":"Do
  X","mode":"manual","model":"claude-opus-4-6","modelProvider":"openclaude"}'
      ```
  - What does Docker do for models — does it just spin up terminals?
    - No. DockerRunner spins up a container process that runs the agent CLI
  binary inside the container (not multiple host terminals). Each agent session
  is a separate child process (container or host). If you run multiple agents
  concurrently, you get multiple child processes/containers — not N terminal
  windows in your host shell. The UI/viewer connects to the streamed
  stdout/stderr JSON events from those processes.
  - Can we use `open-claude-code` / `openclaude` instead of the current Claude
  Code CLI?
    - Yes — adapters were added to spawn `occ` or `openclaude` if present (or to
  fallback to `npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code` / `npx @gitlawb/openclaude` if the
  binaries are not installed globally). If you prefer the router behavior of
  `openclaude` (it manages provider profiles and model routing), you can set
  `modelProvider: "openclaude"` and let OpenClaude handle underlying provider
  selection and routing.
  - Choosing models by tool/availability
    - Both projects support CLI flags like `-m/--model` (we pass `--model` via
  `buildClaudeArgs`) so selecting a model name works. `openclaude` has provider
  profiles and routing (`agentModels`/`agentRouting` in settings), which makes
  mapping agents/tools → models straightforward if you run `openclaude` as the
  router.
    - If you want the Dev Squad to automatically choose models by which tool is
  used, we should implement model discovery + routing (see next steps).
  
  What I researched about the external projects (key points)
  - ruvnet/open-claude-code (occ):
    - CLI name: `occ` (or `npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code ...`).
    - Provides CLI parity with Claude Code (flags like `-m/--model`, `/model` in
  REPL).
    - Quick-start supports `npx` usage so our adapters can fall back to `npx`.
  - GitLawb/openclaude:
    - CLI name: `openclaude` (or `npx @gitlawb/openclaude`).
    - Supports provider profiles, agent routing (`agentModels`/`agentRouting`),
  local gRPC headless server, and many backends (OpenAI-compatible, Ollama,
  etc.). This makes it a good “router” option — run `openclaude` and ask it to
  route to the right provider/model.
    - It supports headless gRPC and a CLI client, so it can run as a local
  router service.
  - How they know which models are available:
    - openclaude can learn model availability from provider configs (env vars,
  saved profiles, Ollama/LM servers) and may expose APIs/commands to probe
  providers.
    - Occ exposes similar CLI flags (REPL slash commands) but programmatic model
  discovery depends on the provider (OpenAI/OpenRouter endpoints or local
  servers).
    - Conclusion: model discovery is provider-specific. We can implement
  adapter-specific discovery (call provider's API, query Ollama, query
  openclaude/occ subcommands or config files).
  
  Recommended, well-designed plan (what I propose to implement next)
  1. Model discovery + routing (priority)
     - Add optional `listModels()` (or `discoverModels()`) to the `ModelAdapter`
  interface.
     - Implement discovery logic for each adapter:
       - `OpenClaudeAdapter`: query openclaude config/profile or call its
  gRPC/CLI listing endpoint (if available).
       - `OpenClaudeCodeAdapter`: attempt CLI-based listing or consult
  environment.
       - `ClaudeCliAdapter`: fallback to reading `.claude/settings.json` or env
  vars.
     - Add server endpoint `GET /api/models?provider=<id>` that returns
  available models for the provider.
     - Add config-driven routing:
       - Parse `.claude/settings.json` (or `~/.claude/settings.json`)
  `agentModels` and `agentRouting` keys for agent→model/provider routing.
  2. UI integration (frontend)
     - Add provider + model dropdowns in the web UI (fetch `/api/models`), let
  users choose per-request, and persist preferred selection.
     - Add a project-level setting to define routing rules; UI to edit
  `.claude/settings.json` in the project.
  3. Docker support
     - Provide a buildable image variant (e.g., `dev-squad-agent:openclaude`)
  with `openclaude`/`occ` preinstalled, or add mount-based runtime selection.
  Provide a documented Dockerfile edit or new Dockerfile to install
  `@ruvnet/open-claude-code` or `@gitlawb/openclaude`.
     - Make `buildDockerArgs` configurable (env var `CONTAINER_AGENT_CMD`) to
  allow running `occ`/`openclaude` instead of `claude` inside the container.
  4. Direct LM Studio / Ollama / local backends
     - Add adapters for Ollama/LM Studio if you want local model servers used
  directly (these typically expose REST/gRPC endpoints we can call).
  
  Quick commands / how to try now
  - Install occ or openclaude on your host (or rely on npx fallback):
    - occ:
      ```bash
      npm install -g @ruvnet/open-claude-code
      # or use npx: npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code "hello"
      ```
    - openclaude:
      ```bash
      npm install -g @gitlawb/openclaude
      # or use npx: npx @gitlawb/openclaude
      ```
  - Force the runner to prefer openclaude over claude on the host (env var):
    ```bash
    export PIPELINE_PREFERRED_PROVIDERS="openclaude,occ,claude-cli"
    ```
  - Example API call including provider:
    ```bash
    curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/chat \
      -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
      -d '{"agent":"A","message":"Explain
  X","mode":"manual","model":"claude-opus-4-6","modelProvider":"openclaude"}'
    ```
  - To make Docker use `openclaude`/`occ`, we need to build a custom agent image
  (example edit to Dockerfile.agent: add `npm install -g
  @ruvnet/open-claude-code @gitlawb/openclaude`) and either:
    - set `DOCKER_IMAGE` to your custom image name, or
    - change `buildDockerArgs` to use a different binary inside the container.

  Progress: I added persistent server logging, a health endpoint, provider/model
  APIs, UI provider selection, and a test that verifies logging — and I ran the
  test to confirm it works.
  
  What I changed and validated
  - Server logging utility: server-logging.ts — appends errors to
  logs/server-errors.log.
  - Chat route: route.ts — now logs unexpected errors to the persistent log and
  always returns JSON on exceptions (prevents client JSON parse failure).
  - Health endpoint: `GET /api/health` -> route.ts.
  - Provider list endpoint: `GET /api/providers` -> route.ts (reports known
  providers and availability).
  - Models endpoint: `GET /api/models?provider=...` -> route.ts (best-effort
  discovery with sensible defaults).
  - Client hook: use-pipeline.ts — now accepts an optional provider and includes
  `modelProvider` in `/api/chat` requests.
  - UI: page.tsx and page.tsx — added provider dropdown (fetches
  `/api/providers`), passes selected provider into `usePipelineState`, and shows
  provider + model pickers in Manual mode.
  - Test script: test-chat-logging.mjs — simulates a failing request and prints
  the server log tail.
  - Verified: Ran `npx tsx scripts/test-chat-logging.mjs`. It produced console
  output showing the error was caught and the log file contains the error stack.
  
  How to reproduce locally (what I ran)
  - Type-check:
  ```bash
  npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json
  ```
  - Run the test that simulates the parse error and inspects the log:
  ```bash
  npx tsx scripts/test-chat-logging.mjs
  ```
  You should see the simulated error and the last lines of server-errors.log. I
  ran this and confirmed the log was written.

  adapter discovery, but adapters currently do not implement a discoverModels
  method — next, implement discovery in adapters (call provider CLIs or read
  env/configs) for accurate model lists. I marked that task in_progress.

  agent image with those CLIs preinstalled or mount host binaries; I left this
  as pending.
  - UI: The provider dropdown uses `/api/providers` so it reflects availability;
  we can also wire the models list to be fetched from
  `/api/models?provider=<id>` whenever a provider is selected and populate the
  model dropdown dynamically.

  logging (payload snippets, headers) when needed — tell me if you want more
  detail captured.

  What I changed and verified
  - Adapter discovery: added optional discoverModels() and improved parsing
  heuristics for occ, openclaude, and claude CLI adapters so they return a
  best-effort model list (or an empty list when discovery ran but found none).
  - API: /api/models now returns { provider, models, modelCount, usedDiscovery,
  fallbackUsed } and returns an empty models array when discovery explicitly
  found none (so the UI can show "no models available").
  - UI: page.tsx and page.tsx
    - Fetch /api/models?provider=... when provider changes.
    - Always repopulate the model dropdown; when no models are returned show a
  disabled "No models available" placeholder and set model="".
    - Added a small banner next to the provider picker that shows the number of
  actual models available.
  - Docker: added Dockerfile.agent.occ (variant image that installs
  @ruvnet/open-claude-code and @gitlawb/openclaude). Runner already supports
  overriding the container image/entrypoint via env vars:
    - PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_IMAGE — set to the agent image name
    - PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_CMD — override container command (e.g.
  "/usr/local/share/npm-global/bin/occ")
  - Robustness:
    - `usePipelineState` sendChat now depends on provider so provider changes
  are honored when sending chat.
    - The chat route still returns JSON and logs server errors so clients won't
  crash with Unexpected end of JSON input.

    - TypeScript compile: npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json
    - Chat logging test: npx tsx test-chat-logging.mjs — confirmed server
  logging behavior
    - Models test: npx tsx test-models.mjs <provider> — exercised discovery &
  empty-discovery paths (e.g., test with provider 'occ' and 'openai-http').

  - Type-check:

  ```bash

  - Inspect models discovery for a provider (e.g., occ, openai-http):
  ```bash

  npx tsx scripts/test-models.mjs openai-http
  ```
  - Build the Docker agent image that includes occ/openclaude:
  ```bash
  docker build -t dev-squad-agent:occ -f pipeline/Dockerfile.agent.occ pipeline/
  # then set this image for the runner:
  export PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_IMAGE=dev-squad-agent:occ
  # optionally set the agent command (if needed)
  export PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_CMD="/usr/local/share/npm-global/bin/occ"
  ```
  
  How the UI handles no-models, errors, and repopulation
  - If the adapter reports models (discovery succeeded), the dropdown is
  populated with those model ids and the banner shows the model count.
  - If discovery ran and returned an empty list, the API returns models: []
  (usedDiscovery=true) and the UI replaces the dropdown with a single disabled
  option "No models available" and selects model="".
  - If discovery did not run/was unavailable, the API falls back to env/default
  models and the UI will show those as normal (fallbackUsed=true is present in
  the API response).
  - The client does defensive parsing for responses and shows consistent
  behavior even when the server returns errors or empty bodies.
  
  About AWS Bedrock support
  - Both ruvnet/open-claude-code (occ) and GitLawb/openclaude document Bedrock
  support in their README — occ includes examples like:
    occ -m bedrock/claude-sonnet "hello"
    and openclaude lists Bedrock / Vertex support via env var configuration.
  - In short: yes — both projects can route to Bedrock when configured with AWS
  credentials and the appropriate model identifier (they expose provider routing
  and provider profiles). Use the occ/openclaude CLI provider flags or
  environment variables as documented in their READMEs.
  
  Notes, caveats, and next suggestions
  - Discovery heuristics are best-effort; CLI outputs vary widely. I improved
  parsing but some noise may still appear for particular CLI versions; we can
  further tighten parsing per-CLI if you want exact behavior (e.g., call
  dedicated JSON flags if the CLI supports them).
  - Docker isolation: the new agent image installs occ/openclaude, but auth into
  cloud providers inside containers (Bedrock, Anthropic) may still require
  mounting credential files or passing env vars — the runner already mounts
  known credential file locations for Claude; you may need to adjust mounting
  for Bedrock (AWS credentials) or use Docker secrets.
  - Pipeline mode still uses the pipeline-run default model for
  supervisor/concept flows (claude-opus-4-6 in the current flow). If you want
  the selected model to be applied to pipeline runs as well, I can wire the
  pipeline-start flow to accept and persist a chosen model/provider.
  - If you want stricter discovery behavior (e.g., only show discovered models
  and never fall back), I can add a query param like ?strictDiscovery=1 to
  /api/models for manual testing.

  LM Studio and OpenAI‑compatible endpoints — how to wire and repair
  - If LM Studio exposes an OpenAI-compatible HTTP API, reuse the existing
  OpenAI HTTP adapter by setting environment variables:
    - OPENAI_BASE_URL — e.g. http://localhost:8000 or
  http://host.docker.internal:8000 (Docker)
    - OPENAI_MODEL — model id/name LM Studio serves
    - OPENAI_API_KEY — if LM Studio requires a key
  - Quick test (example; adjust endpoint path if LM Studio uses
  /v1/chat/completions vs /v1/completions):
  ```bash
  export OPENAI_BASE_URL="http://localhost:8000"
  export OPENAI_MODEL="your-model-name"
  export OPENAI_API_KEY="(if-required)"
  curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" \
    -d
  '{"model":"'"$OPENAI_MODEL"'","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"hello"}]}'
  \
    $OPENAI_BASE_URL/v1/chat/completions
  ```
  - If LM Studio does NOT implement OpenAI routes, add a small adapter:
    - Create `src/lib/modelAdapters/lmStudioAdapter.ts` copying
  openaiHttpAdapter.ts.
    - Implement discoverModels(): try GET $BASE_URL/v1/models and fallback to
  env OPENAI_MODEL.
    - Implement request send() to match the LM Studio API paths.
  
  Docker notes
  - If the runner uses a container, LM Studio running on the host must be
  reachable from the container. Use:
    - host.docker.internal (mac/windows), or
    - `--network host` (linux), or
    - expose and forward the host port and set OPENAI_BASE_URL to the
  container-reachable address.
  - The new agent image variant I added (pipeline/Dockerfile.agent.occ) installs
  occ/openclaude; for LM Studio you only need the correct OPENAI_BASE_URL env
  forwarded into the container.
  
  Troubleshooting / repair checklist
  1. Is the LM Studio server running and listening on the correct port? curl the
  base URL.
  2. Is the correct base path used (/v1/chat/completions vs /v1/completions)?
  Check LM Studio docs.
  3. Is an API key required? Ensure OPENAI_API_KEY matches.
  4. For Docker, use host.docker.internal or host networking so the container
  can reach the host server.
  5. Run discovery test locally: npx tsx test-models.mjs openai-http — it will
  show usedDiscovery/fallbackUsed and modelCount.
  6. Check logs: tail -n 200 server-errors.log for adapter/route errors.
  7. For CLI-based providers (occ/openclaude), ensure the binary is on PATH or
  rely on npx fallback. If a CLI is missing, either install it globally or let
  the runner use npx.

  How Bedrock/Claude Code works with this
  - occ/openclaude/claude-cli can route to AWS Bedrock when the CLI is
  configured with AWS credentials or appropriate provider config. Discovery will
  only list Bedrock models if the CLI (or LM Studio server) can access Bedrock
  (i.e., AWS env vars, shared credentials, or mounted credential files inside
  Docker).
  - To discover Bedrock models automatically we need credentials present in the
  environment where discovery runs (host or container). The adapters call the
  CLIs (local or npx) and will return whatever the CLI reports. If you want me
  to probe Bedrock models here, provide your AWS credentials in the environment
  or point a container at your credential files.

  responses from HTTP-backed providers (LM Studio/OpenAI) as a

  Quick update — I fixed the “models appear then disappear” behavior and added a
  manual refresh + status so you won’t be surprised when discovery runs
  afterward.
  
  What happened and what I changed
  - Cause: the client showed initial fallback model options immediately, then
  the background discovery call completed and returned an explicit empty result;
  the UI replaced the list with a “No models available” placeholder — that
  produced the oscillation you saw.
  - Fixes applied:
    - Preserve existing non-empty model options if discovery runs and returns an
  empty list (so the dropdown won’t vanish unexpectedly).
    - Record discovery metadata (usedDiscovery/modelCount/fallbackUsed) and show
  a small status next to the model count: e.g. “(discovery found 0 — using
  fallback)”, “(discovered N)”, or “(fallback)”.
    - Added a Refresh button next to the provider/model UI so you can re-run
  discovery on demand.
    - Implemented these changes in both Office (page.tsx) and Squad (page.tsx)
  views.
    - Added `fetchModelsForProvider()` functions that do the same discovery
  logic the effect uses (so Refresh works).
  - Other related work in this session:
    - LM Studio adapter added (discovery via /v1/models) and registered.
    - UI selections (provider + model) are now included in pipeline starts and
  persisted into staging state so orchestrator/runner can use the selected
  model/provider.
    - Ran TypeScript check and the repository tests used here:
      - npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json
      - npx tsx test-models.mjs occ|openclaude|openai-http|lm-studio
      - npx tsx test-chat-logging.mjs
  
  How this feels now (what you’ll see)
  - Immediately after choosing Manual + a provider, you’ll keep the existing
  model list (fallback) instead of it disappearing if discovery later returns
  empty.
  - A small inline status will indicate whether discovery ran and what it
  returned.
  - If discovery returned 0 models you’ll see: (discovery found 0 — using
  fallback). Use the new Refresh button to re-run discovery manually.
  - If discovery finds models, the dropdown will update to those discovered
  models.

  User: what are these upload things your talking about what are you talking
  abou about the embedding? 
  
  GitHub Copilot: Short answer: embeddings are numeric vectors that represent
  the meaning of text. "Upload" means computing those vectors for each document
  and storing them in a vector database so you can do fast semantic similarity
  searches (the core of RAG).
  
  How it works (high level)
  - You convert documents into a JSONL of records (we produced
  `devsquad_memory.jsonl` via ingest_and_embed.py).  
  - For each record you call an embedding model (OpenAI, a local model,
  sentence-transformers, Ollama, etc.) to get a numeric vector.  
  - You store (upsert) those vectors + metadata into a vector DB (Chroma,
  Pinecone, Weaviate, LanceDB, Milvus, or even a local file). That storing step
  is what I meant by "upload".
  
  Minimal examples
  
  1) Generate JSONL (if you haven't already):
  ```bash
  python3 tools/rag/ingest_and_embed.py --source /memories/devSqauCodeMemory
  --out devsquad_memory.jsonl
  ```
  
  2) Create embeddings with OpenAI (example):
  ```python
  # pip install openai
  import os, json, openai
  openai.api_key = os.environ['OPENAI_API_KEY']
  out = []
  with open('devsquad_memory.jsonl') as f:
      for line in f:
          doc = json.loads(line)
          emb = openai.Embedding.create(model='text-embedding-3-small',
  input=doc['text'])
          vec = emb['data'][0]['embedding']
          out.append({'id': doc['id'], 'vector': vec, 'meta': {'path':
  doc['path']}})
  # then upsert `out` into your vector DB (or write to disk)
  ```
  
  3) Or create embeddings locally (no cloud key):
  ```bash
  pip install sentence-transformers
  ```
  ```python
  from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
  import json
  model = SentenceTransformer('all-MiniLM-L6-v2')
  with open('devsquad_memory.jsonl') as f:
      for line in f:
          doc = json.loads(line)
          vec = model.encode(doc['text']).tolist()
          # save vec + metadata locally or insert into a local vector DB
  ```
  
  Security notes (important)
  - Never upload secrets (API keys, tokens, `.claude/settings.json`) to external
  providers. Inspect and redact `devsquad_memory.jsonl` first.  
  - You can keep vectors local (Chroma, LanceDB) if you want no third-party
  upload.
  
  If you want, I can:
  - Run the OpenAI embedding pass (need `OPENAI_API_KEY`), or  
  - Run a local embedding pass with `sentence-transformers` and produce a local
  vector file/Chroma collection.

  What I did (concise)
  - Created/updated devSquadMemory/ and moved all research files there.
  - Added a workspace-local venv at .venv and installed sentence-transformers +
  numpy.

  - Recreate venv and install deps:
    pip3 install --user -r requirements.txt
    or use the workspace venv:
    python3 -m venv .venv
    source activate
    pip install -r requirements.txt
  
  - Rebuild embeddings (if needed):
    python3 build_local_embeddings.py
  
  - Query the index:
    python3 query_helper.py --query "how does the runner spawn claude" --topk 5

  Done — your chatbots will now automatically use the workspace vector memory.
  
  Quick summary of what I implemented
  - Integrated local RAG into the chat endpoints used by your bots:
    - Added a Python helper to compute query embeddings: query_embed.py
    - Added a Node retriever that loads the generated vectors and returns top-k
  matches: localRetriever.ts
    - Updated the chat handler: route.ts now calls the retriever (manual and
  pipeline flows) and prepends retrieved snippets to the prompt before spawning
  `claude`.
  - The retrieval flow is self-contained in the workspace:
    - .venv — virtualenv with sentence-transformers installed
    - workspace_docs.jsonl and workspace_vectors.jsonl — docs + vectors (39
  chunks)
    - build_local_embeddings.py / query_helper.py for rebuilding / querying
  locally
  - Runtime details:
    - The retriever spawns the workspace Python helper to compute the query
  embedding (so semantic embeddings match the document vectors).
    - It returns top-5 matches; snippets are limited to ~800 chars and are
  prepended inside a [RETRIEVED SOURCES] block.
    - Retrieval is best-effort; if it fails the chat continues normally.
    - You can disable RAG at runtime by setting DISABLE_RAG=1 in the
  environment.
  
  How you can test it locally
  - Rebuild embeddings (if needed):
    python3 -m venv .venv
    source activate
    pip install -r requirements.txt
    python3 build_local_embeddings.py
  
  - Test query locally:
    python3 query_helper.py --query "how does the runner spawn claude" --topk 5
  
  - Test the chat endpoint (example curl):
    curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/chat -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" -d '{"agent":"A","message":"How does the runner spawn
  claude?","mode":"manual","model":"claude-sonnet-4-6"}'
  
  Notes and config you can change
  - Top-k and snippet length are configurable in localRetriever.ts and in the
  prompt-building in src/app/api/chat/route.ts.
  - If you prefer the retriever to run only for certain agents or phases, I can
  modify the chat handler to be selective.
  - If you want the retrieval to be done via a persistent local service (instead
  of spawning Python per query), I can add a lightweight HTTP microservice for
  that.
  
  Next steps (optional)
  - Export vectors into a local vector DB (Chroma/LanceDB) and replace JSONL
  scans with DB queries for larger scale.
  - Add attribution mapping to include which snippet influenced an assistant
  claim.
  - Make RAG injection configurable via UI controls (top-k, enable/disable per
  agent).
  
  Would you like me to:
  A) Tune top-k or snippet length default,  
  B) Limit RAG to certain agents/phases, or  
  C) Add a persistent local retrieval service (HTTP) for faster queries?

  - A small persistent HTTP microservice (recommended: Python + FastAPI) that:
    - Loads the precomputed vectors in workspace_vectors.jsonl into memory on
  startup (numpy array or Faiss index).
    - Loads the same sentence-transformers model used to build embeddings so
  query embeddings match document vectors.
    - Exposes endpoints:
      - POST /query — accepts { query, top_k } and returns top-k results with
  score, text, and metadata.
      - POST /reindex — optional: rebuild vectors from workspace_docs.jsonl.
      - GET /health — liveness/readiness.
    - Listens on localhost by default and is configured via env vars
  (RETRIEVER_HOST, RETRIEVER_PORT, RETRIEVER_URL).
  
  Data flow (how it works)
  1. You start the retriever service (loads vectors and model into RAM; optional
  Faiss index built).
  2. Chat flow (route.ts) calls localRetriever.ts.
  3. `localRetriever` sends an HTTP POST to the retriever: { query, top_k }.
  4. Retriever encodes the query, computes cosine similarity against the
  in-memory vector matrix, returns top-k doc objects.
  5. `localRetriever` formats the returned snippets and the chat handler injects
  them into the prompt exactly as the current RAG injection logic.
  6. If the retriever is unreachable, `localRetriever` falls back to the
  previous method (spawn Python helper per query) so behavior is robust.
  
  Endpoint contract (example)
  - Request POST /query
    {
      "query": "How does the runner spawn claude?",
      "top_k": 5
    }
  - Response
    {
      "query":"How does the runner spawn claude?",
      "top_k":5,
      "results":[
        { "id":"doc-12", "text":"...snippet...",
  "metadata":{"source":"file.md","loc":[10,120]}, "score":0.923 },
        ...
      ]
    }
  
  Implementation notes / choices
  - Language: Python recommended because you already have
  build_local_embeddings.py and sentence-transformers in the project.
  - Index: For <100k vectors, numpy argmax over dot products is fine. For larger
  corpora, add Faiss for ANN and faster queries.
  - Model: Use the same model name/version used when creating
  `workspace_vectors.jsonl` to avoid embedding mismatch.
  - Security: Bind to 127.0.0.1 by default; use token/TLS if exposing
  externally.
  - Config: Node side reads RETRIEVER_URL (default http://127.0.0.1:8001). Also
  expose RETRIEVER_TOP_K and RAG_SNIPPET_LEN env vars for tuning.
  
  Integration changes (what files will change)
  - Add file: `devSquadMemory/retriever_service.py` (FastAPI server that loads
  vectors and serves /query).
  - Update: localRetriever.ts to call `process.env.RETRIEVER_URL` and send POST
  /query; implement fallback to current spawn-Python code if the service is
  down.
  - Update: route.ts only to respect new env config (if needed).
  
  Quick start commands (to run the service locally)
  ```bash
  python3 -m venv devSquadMemory/.venv
  source devSquadMemory/.venv/bin/activate
  pip install -r devSquadMemory/requirements.txt   # ensure fastapi, uvicorn,
  sentence-transformers, numpy (and faiss-cpu if desired)
  # run
  uvicorn devSquadMemory.retriever_service:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8001
  ```
  
  Example TypeScript client (inside localRetriever.ts)
  - Use Node fetch to call the service and return the results; fall back to the
  existing spawn-Python helper if fetch fails.
  
  Fallback behavior
  - If the retriever HTTP call times out or errors, `localRetriever` should log
  a warning and fall back to the current process-per-query approach so chat
  continues uninterrupted.
  
  Optional enhancements
  - Add /reindex endpoint to rebuild vectors on demand.
  - Add caching (LRU) for recent queries.
  - Add metrics (request counts, latencies).
  - Replace JSONL matrix with Faiss or a small vector DB if you outgrow
  in-memory arrays.

  The switch-hook lives in two places:
  
  - Runner/wrapper that currently spawns Claude: runner.ts — look for
  `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` and the HostRunner.spawn() block that logs a
  fallback to `claude` (the "requested provider ... falling back to claude-cli"
  warning).
  - Adapter interface + stubs: ModelAdapter.ts, claudeCliAdapter.ts,
  openaiHttpAdapter.ts and README.md (explains how to add adapters).
  
  How it’s intended to work
  - `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` is a hint; runner should resolve a
  ModelAdapter for that provider and call its `spawn()` instead of directly
  invoking `claude`.
  - The `ModelAdapter` interface defines `isAvailable()` and `spawn(...)`.
  Adapters (HTTP-backed or local CLIs) implement this and return a
  ChildProcess-like stream that the rest of the pipeline consumes.
  - To enable other providers you add an adapter (or an adapter factory, e.g.
  `src/lib/modelAdapters/index.ts`), then wire runner.ts to lookup the adapter
  by `opts.modelProvider`, call `adapter.spawn({ args: buildClaudeArgs(opts),
  cwd: ..., env: ... })` when available, and fall back to the existing `claude`
  CLI otherwise.

  Summary: I added adapters for ruvnet/open-claude-code (occ) and
  Gitlawb/openclaude, updated the adapter factory and runner wiring, ran a
  type-check, and verified npx is available on your machine for fallback.
  
  What I changed (files)
  - Added: openClaudeCodeAdapter.ts
  - Added: openClaudeAdapter.ts
  - Updated: index.ts (maps 'occ' / 'open-claude-code' / 'openclaude' to
  adapters)
  - Runner wiring (already added earlier): runner.ts uses adapters when
  `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` is set
  
  How Dev Squad currently uses the Claude Code CLI
  - The pipeline spawns a Claude CLI process via runner.ts. Host runs call
  `claude` directly; Docker runs start a container that executes the `claude`
  binary inside the image.
  - The runner builds CLI args with `buildClaudeArgs` /
  `buildContainerClaudeArgs` (permission-mode, model, output-format=stream-json,
  system-prompt, roleFile, etc.).
  - route.ts and orchestrator.ts call `createRunner()` and
  `runner.spawn(runnerOpts)` to start sessions; `streamClaude` consumes the
  CLI's JSONL streaming output and records events.
  - The pipeline uses `.claude/settings.json`, `.claude/hooks`, and Docker
  credential mounting logic (see `resolveDockerCredentialBootstrap`) to manage
  credentials and hooks.
  
  What I implemented for open-claude-code / openclaude
  - HostRunner: when `RunnerOptions.modelProvider` is present and an adapter is
  available, the runner uses the adapter's `spawn()` instead of directly
  invoking `claude`.
  - Adapters:
    - `OpenClaudeCodeAdapter`: spawns `occ` if installed, otherwise falls back
  to `npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code [args]`.
    - `OpenClaudeAdapter`: spawns `openclaude` if installed, otherwise falls
  back to `npx @gitlawb/openclaude [args]`.
  - Type-check passed locally.
  
  Environment check (on your machine)
  - I checked available CLIs: `occ` and `openclaude` were NOT on PATH, but `npx`
  is available — the adapters will fall back to `npx` to run the packages if you
  haven't installed them globally.
  
  How to use these adapters now
  - Set the provider in the RunnerOptions you pass to `runner.spawn`:
    - modelProvider: 'occ' or 'open-claude-code' — uses ruvnet/open-claude-code
  (occ)
    - modelProvider: 'openclaude' — uses Gitlawb/openclaude
  - Example (quick programmatic test — run from repo root with tsx or
  node/ts-node):
    - Create a small script (e.g., `scripts/test-host-adapter.mjs`) containing:
      ```js
      import { createRunner } from '../pipeline/runner.ts';
      const runner = createRunner('host');
      const child = runner.spawn({
        prompt: 'Reply with exactly OK and nothing else.',
        projectDir: process.cwd(),
        systemPrompt: 'You are a test assistant.',
        model: 'claude-sonnet-4-6',
        modelProvider: 'occ', // or 'openclaude'
      });
      child.stdout.on('data', (c) => console.log('[OUT]', c.toString()));
      child.stderr.on('data', (c) => console.error('[ERR]', c.toString()));
      child.on('close', (code) => console.log('EXIT', code));
      ```
    - Run:
      ```bash
      npx tsx scripts/test-host-adapter.mjs
      ```
  - If you prefer the web/API path, you can pass `modelProvider` in the
  `RunnerOptions` used by `streamClaude`. (Currently the UI/post handler doesn't
  accept a modelProvider param from the request body by default — you can add it
  where `streamClaude` is invoked, e.g. in route.ts or set
  `process.env.PIPELINE_MODEL_PROVIDER` and include it when building the
  RunnerOptions.)
  
  Docker note / caveat
  - DockerRunner still invokes the `claude` binary inside the container image
  (`dev-squad-agent:latest`). To use `occ` or `openclaude` inside Docker you
  must:
    - Use a container image that has `occ` or `openclaude` installed, and/or
    - Mount the host binary into the container, or
    - Update `buildDockerArgs` to run the adapter's binary path inside the
  container (and ensure the binary exists there).
  - For now the fastest path is to use the HostRunner (createRunner('host') or
  let AutoRunner fall back to host) so the adapters can spawn host-installed
  CLIs or run via `npx`.
  
  Recommended next steps (I can do these if you want)
  - Add a small API parameter to accept `modelProvider` from the chat POST body
  and propagate it to `streamClaude` (makes UI-driven selection trivial).
  - Add Docker support so DockerRunner can run `occ`/`openclaude` (build a
  custom image or change the invoked binary in `buildDockerArgs`).
  - Optionally add provider-specific integration (credentials, provider
  profiles) for better UX (e.g., pass OPENAI/Anthropic env vars through when
  using openclaude provider).

  Quick progress update — what I changed and what I learned, plus next steps.
  
  What I implemented so far
  - Adapters: added adapters for occ/open-claude-code and openclaude:
    - openClaudeCodeAdapter.ts
    - openClaudeAdapter.ts
    - factory: index.ts
  - Runner wiring:
    - runner.ts HostRunner now:
      - respects an explicit `RunnerOptions.modelProvider`,
      - auto-detects a preferred adapter order (env:
  `PIPELINE_PREFERRED_PROVIDERS`, default: occ → openclaude → claude-cli) when
  none is provided,
      - falls back to the legacy `claude` CLI if nothing else is available.
  - API propagation:
    - route.ts now accepts an optional `modelProvider` in the POST body and
  passes it through to the runner/session creation.
  - Type-checks ran successfully locally (no TypeScript errors).
  
  Short answers to your questions
  - What is the Docker container?
    - The agent container image is `dev-squad-agent:latest` (built from
  Dockerfile.agent). It is a Node image where the official
  `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` CLI is installed by default (`npm install -g
  @anthropic-ai/claude-code`).
  - How does the UI choose models?
    - The API now supports a `modelProvider` field in the POST `/api/chat`
  payload. The UI can send `{ model: "<model-name>", modelProvider:
  "<provider-id>" }`. If `modelProvider` is omitted, the runner will auto-detect
  available adapters (occ/openclaude/claude) in the configured preference order.
    - Example request:
      ```bash
      curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/chat \
        -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
        -d '{"agent":"A","message":"Do
  X","mode":"manual","model":"claude-opus-4-6","modelProvider":"openclaude"}'
      ```
  - What does Docker do for models — does it just spin up terminals?
    - No. DockerRunner spins up a container process that runs the agent CLI
  binary inside the container (not multiple host terminals). Each agent session
  is a separate child process (container or host). If you run multiple agents
  concurrently, you get multiple child processes/containers — not N terminal
  windows in your host shell. The UI/viewer connects to the streamed
  stdout/stderr JSON events from those processes.
  - Can we use `open-claude-code` / `openclaude` instead of the current Claude
  Code CLI?
    - Yes — adapters were added to spawn `occ` or `openclaude` if present (or to
  fallback to `npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code` / `npx @gitlawb/openclaude` if the
  binaries are not installed globally). If you prefer the router behavior of
  `openclaude` (it manages provider profiles and model routing), you can set
  `modelProvider: "openclaude"` and let OpenClaude handle underlying provider
  selection and routing.
  - Choosing models by tool/availability
    - Both projects support CLI flags like `-m/--model` (we pass `--model` via
  `buildClaudeArgs`) so selecting a model name works. `openclaude` has provider
  profiles and routing (`agentModels`/`agentRouting` in settings), which makes
  mapping agents/tools → models straightforward if you run `openclaude` as the
  router.
    - If you want the Dev Squad to automatically choose models by which tool is
  used, we should implement model discovery + routing (see next steps).
  
  What I researched about the external projects (key points)
  - ruvnet/open-claude-code (occ):
    - CLI name: `occ` (or `npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code ...`).
    - Provides CLI parity with Claude Code (flags like `-m/--model`, `/model` in
  REPL).
    - Quick-start supports `npx` usage so our adapters can fall back to `npx`.
  - GitLawb/openclaude:
    - CLI name: `openclaude` (or `npx @gitlawb/openclaude`).
    - Supports provider profiles, agent routing (`agentModels`/`agentRouting`),
  local gRPC headless server, and many backends (OpenAI-compatible, Ollama,
  etc.). This makes it a good “router” option — run `openclaude` and ask it to
  route to the right provider/model.
    - It supports headless gRPC and a CLI client, so it can run as a local
  router service.
  - How they know which models are available:
    - openclaude can learn model availability from provider configs (env vars,
  saved profiles, Ollama/LM servers) and may expose APIs/commands to probe
  providers.
    - Occ exposes similar CLI flags (REPL slash commands) but programmatic model
  discovery depends on the provider (OpenAI/OpenRouter endpoints or local
  servers).
    - Conclusion: model discovery is provider-specific. We can implement
  adapter-specific discovery (call provider's API, query Ollama, query
  openclaude/occ subcommands or config files).
  
  Recommended, well-designed plan (what I propose to implement next)
  1. Model discovery + routing (priority)
     - Add optional `listModels()` (or `discoverModels()`) to the `ModelAdapter`
  interface.
     - Implement discovery logic for each adapter:
       - `OpenClaudeAdapter`: query openclaude config/profile or call its
  gRPC/CLI listing endpoint (if available).
       - `OpenClaudeCodeAdapter`: attempt CLI-based listing or consult
  environment.
       - `ClaudeCliAdapter`: fallback to reading `.claude/settings.json` or env
  vars.
     - Add server endpoint `GET /api/models?provider=<id>` that returns
  available models for the provider.
     - Add config-driven routing:
       - Parse `.claude/settings.json` (or `~/.claude/settings.json`)
  `agentModels` and `agentRouting` keys for agent→model/provider routing.
  2. UI integration (frontend)
     - Add provider + model dropdowns in the web UI (fetch `/api/models`), let
  users choose per-request, and persist preferred selection.
     - Add a project-level setting to define routing rules; UI to edit
  `.claude/settings.json` in the project.
  3. Docker support
     - Provide a buildable image variant (e.g., `dev-squad-agent:openclaude`)
  with `openclaude`/`occ` preinstalled, or add mount-based runtime selection.
  Provide a documented Dockerfile edit or new Dockerfile to install
  `@ruvnet/open-claude-code` or `@gitlawb/openclaude`.
     - Make `buildDockerArgs` configurable (env var `CONTAINER_AGENT_CMD`) to
  allow running `occ`/`openclaude` instead of `claude` inside the container.
  4. Direct LM Studio / Ollama / local backends
     - Add adapters for Ollama/LM Studio if you want local model servers used
  directly (these typically expose REST/gRPC endpoints we can call).
  
  Quick commands / how to try now
  - Install occ or openclaude on your host (or rely on npx fallback):
    - occ:
      ```bash
      npm install -g @ruvnet/open-claude-code
      # or use npx: npx @ruvnet/open-claude-code "hello"
      ```
    - openclaude:
      ```bash
      npm install -g @gitlawb/openclaude
      # or use npx: npx @gitlawb/openclaude
      ```
  - Force the runner to prefer openclaude over claude on the host (env var):
    ```bash
    export PIPELINE_PREFERRED_PROVIDERS="openclaude,occ,claude-cli"
    ```
  - Example API call including provider:
    ```bash
    curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/chat \
      -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
      -d '{"agent":"A","message":"Explain
  X","mode":"manual","model":"claude-opus-4-6","modelProvider":"openclaude"}'
    ```
  - To make Docker use `openclaude`/`occ`, we need to build a custom agent image
  (example edit to Dockerfile.agent: add `npm install -g
  @ruvnet/open-claude-code @gitlawb/openclaude`) and either:
    - set `DOCKER_IMAGE` to your custom image name, or
    - change `buildDockerArgs` to use a different binary inside the container.

  Summary: Added server-side error handling to `/api/chat` so it always returns
  JSON on exceptions, and made the client `sendChat` more robust by checking for
  non-OK and non-JSON responses and surfacing readable errors. This prevents the
  "Unexpected end of JSON input" failure and gives clearer diagnostics if the
  server fails.

  success:false, error } on unexpected exceptions.

  content-type before calling `res.json()`, and throw helpful error messages
  with response text when parsing fails.

  Progress: I added persistent server logging, a health endpoint, provider/model
  APIs, UI provider selection, and a test that verifies logging — and I ran the
  test to confirm it works.
  
  What I changed and validated
  - Server logging utility: server-logging.ts — appends errors to
  logs/server-errors.log.
  - Chat route: route.ts — now logs unexpected errors to the persistent log and
  always returns JSON on exceptions (prevents client JSON parse failure).
  - Health endpoint: `GET /api/health` -> route.ts.
  - Provider list endpoint: `GET /api/providers` -> route.ts (reports known
  providers and availability).
  - Models endpoint: `GET /api/models?provider=...` -> route.ts (best-effort
  discovery with sensible defaults).
  - Client hook: use-pipeline.ts — now accepts an optional provider and includes
  `modelProvider` in `/api/chat` requests.
  - UI: page.tsx and page.tsx — added provider dropdown (fetches
  `/api/providers`), passes selected provider into `usePipelineState`, and shows
  provider + model pickers in Manual mode.
  - Test script: test-chat-logging.mjs — simulates a failing request and prints
  the server log tail.
  - Verified: Ran `npx tsx scripts/test-chat-logging.mjs`. It produced console
  output showing the error was caught and the log file contains the error stack.
  
  How to reproduce locally (what I ran)
  - Type-check:
  ```bash
  npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json
  ```
  - Run the test that simulates the parse error and inspects the log:
  ```bash
  npx tsx scripts/test-chat-logging.mjs
  ```
  You should see the simulated error and the last lines of server-errors.log. I
  ran this and confirmed the log was written.
  
  Notes and next recommended steps
  - Model discovery: I added a models endpoint and a best-effort hook for
  adapter discovery, but adapters currently do not implement a discoverModels
  method — next, implement discovery in adapters (call provider CLIs or read
  env/configs) for accurate model lists. I marked that task in_progress.
  - Docker support: To use `occ`/`openclaude` inside Docker you’ll need a custom
  agent image with those CLIs preinstalled or mount host binaries; I left this
  as pending.
  - UI: The provider dropdown uses `/api/providers` so it reflects availability;
  we can also wire the models list to be fetched from
  `/api/models?provider=<id>` whenever a provider is selected and populate the
  model dropdown dynamically.
  - Logging verbosity: Current server log is minimal; I can add request-context
  logging (payload snippets, headers) when needed — tell me if you want more
  detail captured.

  What I changed and verified
  - Adapter discovery: added optional discoverModels() and improved parsing
  heuristics for occ, openclaude, and claude CLI adapters so they return a
  best-effort model list (or an empty list when discovery ran but found none).
  - API: /api/models now returns { provider, models, modelCount, usedDiscovery,
  fallbackUsed } and returns an empty models array when discovery explicitly
  found none (so the UI can show "no models available").
  - UI: page.tsx and page.tsx
    - Fetch /api/models?provider=... when provider changes.
    - Always repopulate the model dropdown; when no models are returned show a
  disabled "No models available" placeholder and set model="".
    - Added a small banner next to the provider picker that shows the number of
  actual models available.
  - Docker: added Dockerfile.agent.occ (variant image that installs
  @ruvnet/open-claude-code and @gitlawb/openclaude). Runner already supports
  overriding the container image/entrypoint via env vars:
    - PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_IMAGE — set to the agent image name
    - PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_CMD — override container command (e.g.
  "/usr/local/share/npm-global/bin/occ")
  - Robustness:
    - `usePipelineState` sendChat now depends on provider so provider changes
  are honored when sending chat.
    - The chat route still returns JSON and logs server errors so clients won't
  crash with Unexpected end of JSON input.
  - Tests run:
    - TypeScript compile: npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json
    - Chat logging test: npx tsx test-chat-logging.mjs — confirmed server
  logging behavior
    - Models test: npx tsx test-models.mjs <provider> — exercised discovery &
  empty-discovery paths (e.g., test with provider 'occ' and 'openai-http').
  
  Quick commands to reproduce locally
  - Type-check:
  ```bash
  npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json
  ```
  - Run chat logging test:
  ```bash
  npx tsx scripts/test-chat-logging.mjs
  ```
  - Inspect models discovery for a provider (e.g., occ, openai-http):
  ```bash
  npx tsx scripts/test-models.mjs occ
  npx tsx scripts/test-models.mjs openai-http
  ```
  - Build the Docker agent image that includes occ/openclaude:
  ```bash
  docker build -t dev-squad-agent:occ -f pipeline/Dockerfile.agent.occ pipeline/
  # then set this image for the runner:
  export PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_IMAGE=dev-squad-agent:occ
  # optionally set the agent command (if needed)
  export PIPELINE_DOCKER_AGENT_CMD="/usr/local/share/npm-global/bin/occ"
  ```
  
  How the UI handles no-models, errors, and repopulation
  - If the adapter reports models (discovery succeeded), the dropdown is
  populated with those model ids and the banner shows the model count.
  - If discovery ran and returned an empty list, the API returns models: []
  (usedDiscovery=true) and the UI replaces the dropdown with a single disabled
  option "No models available" and selects model="".
  - If discovery did not run/was unavailable, the API falls back to env/default
  models and the UI will show those as normal (fallbackUsed=true is present in
  the API response).
  - The client does defensive parsing for responses and shows consistent
  behavior even when the server returns errors or empty bodies.
  
  About AWS Bedrock support
  - Both ruvnet/open-claude-code (occ) and GitLawb/openclaude document Bedrock
  support in their README — occ includes examples like:
    occ -m bedrock/claude-sonnet "hello"
    and openclaude lists Bedrock / Vertex support via env var configuration.
  - In short: yes — both projects can route to Bedrock when configured with AWS
  credentials and the appropriate model identifier (they expose provider routing
  and provider profiles). Use the occ/openclaude CLI provider flags or
  environment variables as documented in their READMEs.
  
  Notes, caveats, and next suggestions
  - Discovery heuristics are best-effort; CLI outputs vary widely. I improved
  parsing but some noise may still appear for particular CLI versions; we can
  further tighten parsing per-CLI if you want exact behavior (e.g., call
  dedicated JSON flags if the CLI supports them).
  - Docker isolation: the new agent image installs occ/openclaude, but auth into
  cloud providers inside containers (Bedrock, Anthropic) may still require
  mounting credential files or passing env vars — the runner already mounts
  known credential file locations for Claude; you may need to adjust mounting
  for Bedrock (AWS credentials) or use Docker secrets.
  - Pipeline mode still uses the pipeline-run default model for
  supervisor/concept flows (claude-opus-4-6 in the current flow). If you want
  the selected model to be applied to pipeline runs as well, I can wire the
  pipeline-start flow to accept and persist a chosen model/provider.
  - If you want stricter discovery behavior (e.g., only show discovered models
  and never fall back), I can add a query param like ?strictDiscovery=1 to
  /api/models for manual testing.

  LM Studio and OpenAI‑compatible endpoints — how to wire and repair
  - If LM Studio exposes an OpenAI-compatible HTTP API, reuse the existing
  OpenAI HTTP adapter by setting environment variables:
    - OPENAI_BASE_URL — e.g. http://localhost:8000 or
  http://host.docker.internal:8000 (Docker)
    - OPENAI_MODEL — model id/name LM Studio serves
    - OPENAI_API_KEY — if LM Studio requires a key
  - Quick test (example; adjust endpoint path if LM Studio uses
  /v1/chat/completions vs /v1/completions):
  ```bash
  export OPENAI_BASE_URL="http://localhost:8000"
  export OPENAI_MODEL="your-model-name"
  export OPENAI_API_KEY="(if-required)"
  curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" \
    -d
  '{"model":"'"$OPENAI_MODEL"'","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"hello"}]}'
  \
    $OPENAI_BASE_URL/v1/chat/completions
  ```
  - If LM Studio does NOT implement OpenAI routes, add a small adapter:
    - Create `src/lib/modelAdapters/lmStudioAdapter.ts` copying
  openaiHttpAdapter.ts.
    - Implement discoverModels(): try GET $BASE_URL/v1/models and fallback to
  env OPENAI_MODEL.
    - Implement request send() to match the LM Studio API paths.
  
  Docker notes
  - If the runner uses a container, LM Studio running on the host must be
  reachable from the container. Use:
    - host.docker.internal (mac/windows), or
    - `--network host` (linux), or
    - expose and forward the host port and set OPENAI_BASE_URL to the
  container-reachable address.
  - The new agent image variant I added (pipeline/Dockerfile.agent.occ) installs
  occ/openclaude; for LM Studio you only need the correct OPENAI_BASE_URL env
  forwarded into the container.
  
  Troubleshooting / repair checklist
  1. Is the LM Studio server running and listening on the correct port? curl the
  base URL.
  2. Is the correct base path used (/v1/chat/completions vs /v1/completions)?
  Check LM Studio docs.
  3. Is an API key required? Ensure OPENAI_API_KEY matches.
  4. For Docker, use host.docker.internal or host networking so the container
  can reach the host server.
  5. Run discovery test locally: npx tsx test-models.mjs openai-http — it will
  show usedDiscovery/fallbackUsed and modelCount.
  6. Check logs: tail -n 200 server-errors.log for adapter/route errors.
  7. For CLI-based providers (occ/openclaude), ensure the binary is on PATH or
  rely on npx fallback. If a CLI is missing, either install it globally or let
  the runner use npx.

  How Bedrock/Claude Code works with this
  - occ/openclaude/claude-cli can route to AWS Bedrock when the CLI is
  configured with AWS credentials or appropriate provider config. Discovery will
  only list Bedrock models if the CLI (or LM Studio server) can access Bedrock
  (i.e., AWS env vars, shared credentials, or mounted credential files inside
  Docker).
  - To discover Bedrock models automatically we need credentials present in the
  environment where discovery runs (host or container). The adapters call the
  CLIs (local or npx) and will return whatever the CLI reports. If you want me
  to probe Bedrock models here, provide your AWS credentials in the environment
  or point a container at your credential files.

  What happened and what I changed
  - Cause: the client showed initial fallback model options immediately, then
  the background discovery call completed and returned an explicit empty result;
  the UI replaced the list with a “No models available” placeholder — that
  produced the oscillation you saw.
  - Fixes applied:

  empty list (so the dropdown won’t vanish unexpectedly).
    - Record discovery metadata (usedDiscovery/modelCount/fallbackUsed) and show
  a small status next to the model count: e.g. “(discovery found 0 — using
  fallback)”, “(discovered N)”, or “(fallback)”.
    - Added a Refresh button next to the provider/model UI so you can re-run
  discovery on demand.
    - Implemented these changes in both Office (page.tsx) and Squad (page.tsx)
  views.

  logic the effect uses (so Refresh works).
